People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I mean, I probably do sometimes, but I haven’t noticed it as a major thing compared to the getting distracted and feeling restless.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble finishing tasks or projects that you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I have a bunch of things to do, like calling a counselor, cleaning my room, and sending in an application, I kind of don’t know where to start. Then I’ll jump between them and end up with everything only half done.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

If I have a bunch of things to do, like calling a counselor, cleaning my room, and sending in an application, I kind of don’t know where to start. Then I’ll jump between them and end up with everything only half done.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks causing problems for you at work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. I’m trying to move forward with becoming an automotive technician, and I know I can handle the hands-on part, but I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications because I didn’t finish them right or on time. It’s happened enough that I’m kind of stuck instead of making progress.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with distraction and restlessness, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since I was a kid. In elementary school, teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk out of turn, forget homework, or rush through worksheets and make careless mistakes. So yeah, it’s not really new.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or staying on task in quiet environments, like when you're reading or working on a project by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like if I’m watching a repair video or trying to read a tool manual, I can start it fine, but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at it and didn’t actually take anything in. Then I have to rewind it or reread the same part.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really angry or upset for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get frustrated sometimes, but I haven’t noticed myself getting really angry or upset out of nowhere. It’s not something I’d say is a big issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting frustrated?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. I can get frustrated when something isn’t going smoothly, but I wouldn’t say it’s constant or out of nowhere. It’s more just normal frustration, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are as big of a problem for you as getting distracted?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the restlessness is a big problem too. Maybe not always the same as getting distracted, but since I can’t play soccer like I used to, sitting around the house makes me feel like I’m crawling out of my skin. I’ll end up just pacing room to room because I can’t sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, or finishing tasks affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, mostly with my family I guess. They get frustrated because I keep saying I’m going to move forward with becoming an automotive technician, but I miss training deadlines or mess up applications and have to redo them. So I’m kind of stuck, even though I know I could handle the actual hands-on work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks are causing you a lot of stress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, it stresses me out some. I wouldn’t say I’m in emotional pain all the time or anything, but it does get to me when I can’t follow through on things I know I need to do. It’s frustrating and kind of wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with distraction and restlessness in multiple situations, such as at home, at work, or in social settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it happens in a lot of places. The main thing is I get pulled off track by every little sound or thought. Like if I’m trying to work on an application, a car outside, my phone, or just some random idea in my head can send me off doing something else before I even realize it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting pulled off track really easily. Like, I’ll try to work on an application or something, and then a car outside, my phone, or even just some random thought will take me away from it before I even realize I stopped. It’s been making it hard to get things done.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. If I’m sitting with someone, my leg is usually bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen or messing with my keys and not even notice I’m doing it at first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I probably do sometimes, but I haven’t noticed it as a major thing compared to the getting distracted and feeling restless.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble finishing tasks or projects that you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I have a bunch of things to do, like calling a counselor, cleaning my room, and sending in an application, I kind of don’t know where to start. Then I’ll jump between them and end up with everything only half done.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks causing problems for you at work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. I’m trying to move forward with becoming an automotive technician, and I know I can handle the hands-on part, but I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications because I didn’t finish them right or on time. It’s happened enough that I’m kind of stuck instead of making progress.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with distraction and restlessness, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since I was a kid. In elementary school, teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk out of turn, forget homework, or rush through worksheets and make careless mistakes. So yeah, it’s not really new.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or staying on task in quiet environments, like when you're reading or working on a project by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like if I’m watching a repair video or trying to read a tool manual, I can start it fine, but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at it and didn’t actually take anything in. Then I have to rewind it or reread the same part.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really angry or upset for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get frustrated sometimes, but I haven’t noticed myself getting really angry or upset out of nowhere. It’s not something I’d say is a big issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting frustrated?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. I can get frustrated when something isn’t going smoothly, but I wouldn’t say it’s constant or out of nowhere. It’s more just normal frustration, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are as big of a problem for you as getting distracted?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the restlessness is a big problem too. Maybe not always the same as getting distracted, but since I can’t play soccer like I used to, sitting around the house makes me feel like I’m crawling out of my skin. I’ll end up just pacing room to room because I can’t sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, or finishing tasks affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, mostly with my family I guess. They get frustrated because I keep saying I’m going to move forward with becoming an automotive technician, but I miss training deadlines or mess up applications and have to redo them. So I’m kind of stuck, even though I know I could handle the actual hands-on work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks are causing you a lot of stress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, it stresses me out some. I wouldn’t say I’m in emotional pain all the time or anything, but it does get to me when I can’t follow through on things I know I need to do. It’s frustrating and kind of wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with distraction and restlessness in multiple situations, such as at home, at work, or in social settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it happens in a lot of places. The main thing is I get pulled off track by every little sound or thought. Like if I’m trying to work on an application, a car outside, my phone, or just some random idea in my head can send me off doing something else before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is the main issue, or is it more about the restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think the focus part is probably the main thing. Like, I can start watching a repair video or reading a tool manual, and after a few minutes I realize I’ve just been staring at it. Then I have to rewind it or go back because none of it really stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are as bothersome to you as your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Not as much as the focus stuff, I don’t think. It can be annoying, but the focus problems bother me more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep getting pulled off track really easily. Like, I’ll try to work on an application or something, and then a car outside, my phone, or even just some random thought will take me away from it before I even realize I stopped. If I’m sitting with someone, my leg is usually bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen or messing with my keys and not even notice I’m doing it at first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I probably do sometimes, but I haven’t noticed it as a major thing compared to the getting distracted and feeling restless.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble finishing tasks or projects that you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I have a bunch of things to do, like calling a counselor, cleaning my room, and sending in an application, I kind of don’t know where to start. Then I’ll jump between them and end up with everything only half done.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks causing problems for you at work or school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. I’m trying to move forward with becoming an automotive technician, and I know I can handle the hands-on part, but I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications because I didn’t finish them right or on time. It’s happened enough that I’m kind of stuck instead of making progress.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with distraction and restlessness, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since I was a kid. In elementary school, teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk out of turn, forget homework, or rush through worksheets and make careless mistakes. So yeah, it’s not really new.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or staying on task in quiet environments, like when you're reading or working on a project by yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like if I’m watching a repair video or trying to read a tool manual, I can start it fine, but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at it and didn’t actually take anything in. I’ll end up just pacing room to room because I can’t sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, or finishing tasks affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, mostly with my family I guess. Then I have to rewind it or go back because none of it really stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are as bothersome to you as your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Not as much as the focus stuff, I don’t think. But it is there. Like if I’m sitting with someone, my leg’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, or messing with my keys without even noticing I’m doing it. It can be annoying, but the focus problems bother me more.\n\nDoctor: Were you diagnosed with or did you exhibit symptoms of attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did have stuff like that as a kid. Even in elementary school, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk out of turn, forget to bring in homework, or rush through worksheets and make dumb careless mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks caused problems in your friendships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s caused problems, not so much with friendships honestly, but more with moving forward in life. I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications more than once, and it’s kept me from getting closer to becoming an automotive technician. That’s frustrating because I feel like I can handle the hands-on work fine.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks caused significant problems in your romantic relationships or with your family beyond the frustration they feel with your career progress?\n\nPatient: Not really beyond that, no. It’s not something I’d say is a big issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting frustrated?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. I can get frustrated when something isn’t going smoothly, but I wouldn’t say it’s constant or out of nowhere. It’s more just normal frustration, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are as big of a problem for you as getting distracted?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think the restlessness is a big problem too. Maybe not always the same as getting distracted, but since I can’t play soccer like I used to, sitting around the house makes me feel like I’m crawling out of my skin. I’ll end up just pacing room to room because I can’t sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, or finishing tasks affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has, mostly with my family I guess. They get frustrated because I keep saying I’m going to move forward with becoming an automotive technician, but I miss training deadlines or mess up applications and have to redo them. So I’m kind of stuck, even though I know I could handle the actual hands-on work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks are causing you a lot of stress or emotional pain?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, it stresses me out some. I wouldn’t say I’m in emotional pain all the time or anything, but it does get to me when I can’t follow through on things I know I need to do. It’s frustrating and kind of wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with distraction and restlessness in multiple situations, such as at home, at work, or in social settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it happens in a lot of places. The main thing is I get pulled off track by every little sound or thought. Like if I’m trying to work on an application, a car outside, my phone, or just some random idea in my head can send me off doing something else before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is the main issue, or is it more about the restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think the focus part is probably the main thing. Like, I can start watching a repair video or reading a tool manual, and after a few minutes I realize I’ve just been staring at it. Then I have to rewind it or go back because none of it really stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are as bothersome to you as your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Not as much as the focus stuff, I don’t think. But it is there. Like if I’m sitting with someone, my leg’s usually bouncing, or I’m clicking a pen, or messing with my keys without even noticing I’m doing it. It can be annoying, but the focus problems bother me more.\n\nDoctor: Were you diagnosed with or did you exhibit symptoms of attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did have stuff like that as a kid. Even in elementary school, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk out of turn, forget to bring in homework, or rush through worksheets and make dumb careless mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks caused problems in your friendships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s caused problems, not so much with friendships honestly, but more with moving forward in life. I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications more than once, and it’s kept me from getting closer to becoming an automotive technician. That’s frustrating because I feel like I can handle the hands-on work fine.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks caused significant problems in your romantic relationships or with your family beyond the frustration they feel with your career progress?\n\nPatient: Not really beyond that, no. The biggest problem has been me not moving forward. I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications enough times that I’m still not getting closer to becoming an automotive technician, even though I feel like I can handle the hands-on part. That’s what causes most of the tension.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks consistently across different settings, such as home, work, and social situations, for most of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. The main thing is I get pulled off track by every little sound or thought. Like if I’m trying to work on an application, a car outside, my phone, or just some random idea in my head can send me off doing something else before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is the main issue, or is it more about the restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I think the focus part is probably the main thing. Like, I can start watching a repair video or reading a tool manual, and after a few minutes I realize I’ve just been staring at it. Then I have to rewind it or go back because none of it really stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are as bothersome to you as your difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: Not as much as the focus stuff, I don’t think. It can be annoying, but the focus problems bother me more.\n\nDoctor: Were you diagnosed with or did you exhibit symptoms of attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did have stuff like that as a kid. Even in elementary school, teachers were always moving my seat around because I’d talk out of turn, forget to bring in homework, or rush through worksheets and make dumb careless mistakes.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks caused problems in your friendships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s caused problems, not so much with friendships honestly, but more with moving forward in life. I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications more than once, and it’s kept me from getting closer to becoming an automotive technician. That’s frustrating because I feel like I can handle the hands-on work fine.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks caused significant problems in your romantic relationships or with your family beyond the frustration they feel with your career progress?\n\nPatient: Not really beyond that, no. The biggest problem has been me not moving forward. I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications enough times that I’m still not getting closer to becoming an automotive technician, even though I feel like I can handle the hands-on part. That’s what causes most of the tension.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with distraction, restlessness, and finishing tasks consistently across different settings, such as home, work, and social situations, for most of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that.
